<h3><span id="1808.E2.80.931809"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="1808–1809">1808–1809</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: 1808–1809">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Ready for battle, Wellesley left Cork on 12 July 1808 to participate in the war against French forces in the <a href="/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula" title="Iberian Peninsula">Iberian Peninsula</a>, with his skills as a commander tested and developed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENeillands200339_100-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENeillands200339-100">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>According to the historian <a href="/wiki/Robin_Neillands" title="Robin Neillands">Robin Neillands</a>:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Wellesley had by now acquired the experience on which his later successes were founded. He knew about command from the ground up, about the importance of logistics, about campaigning in a hostile environment. He enjoyed political influence and realised the need to maintain support at home. Above all, he had gained a clear idea of how, by setting attainable objectives and relying on his own force and abilities, a campaign could be fought and won.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENeillands200339_100-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENeillands200339-100">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Wellingtons33rd.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Wellingtons33rd.jpg/220px-Wellingtons33rd.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="159" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Wellingtons33rd.jpg/330px-Wellingtons33rd.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Wellingtons33rd.jpg/440px-Wellingtons33rd.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="869" /></a><figcaption>Reenactors of the <a href="/wiki/33rd_Regiment_of_Foot" class="mw-redirect" title="33rd Regiment of Foot">33rd Regiment of Foot</a> Wellington's Redcoats who fought in the Napoleonic Wars, 1812–1815, here showing the standard line 8th Company</figcaption></figure>
<p>Wellesley defeated the French at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Roli%C3%A7a" title="Battle of Roliça">Battle of Roliça</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Vimeiro" title="Battle of Vimeiro">Battle of Vimeiro</a> in 1808<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971148–154_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971148–154-102">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> but was superseded in command immediately after the latter battle. <a href="/wiki/Sir_Hew_Whiteford_Dalrymple,_1st_Baronet" class="mw-redirect" title="Sir Hew Whiteford Dalrymple, 1st Baronet">General Dalrymple</a> then signed the controversial <a href="/wiki/Convention_of_Sintra" class="mw-redirect" title="Convention of Sintra">Convention of Sintra</a>, which stipulated that the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Navy" title="Royal Navy">Royal Navy</a> transport the <a href="/wiki/French_army" class="mw-redirect" title="French army">French army</a> out of <a href="/wiki/Lisbon" title="Lisbon">Lisbon</a> with all their loot, and insisted on the association of the only available government minister, Wellesley.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971148–154_102-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971148–154-102">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
Dalrymple and Wellesley were recalled to Britain to face a Court of Enquiry. Wellesley had agreed to sign the preliminary armistice, but had not signed the convention, and was cleared.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002124_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002124-103">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Simultaneously, Napoleon entered Spain with his veteran troops to put down the revolt; the new commander of the British forces in the Peninsula, <a href="/wiki/John_Moore_(British_soldier)" class="mw-redirect" title="John Moore (British soldier)">Sir John Moore</a>, died during the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Corunna" title="Battle of Corunna">Battle of Corunna</a> in January 1809.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971171_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971171-104">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although overall the land war with France was not going well from a British perspective, the Peninsula was the one theatre where they, with the <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_people" title="Portuguese people">Portuguese</a>, had provided strong resistance against France and her allies. This contrasted with the disastrous <a href="/wiki/Walcheren_expedition" class="mw-redirect" title="Walcheren expedition">Walcheren expedition</a>, which was typical of the mismanaged British operations of the time. Wellesley submitted a memorandum to <a href="/wiki/Lord_Castlereagh" class="mw-redirect" title="Lord Castlereagh">Lord Castlereagh</a> on the defence of Portugal. He stressed its mountainous frontiers and advocated Lisbon as the main base because the Royal Navy could help to defend it. Castlereagh and the cabinet approved the memo and appointed him head of all British forces in Portugal.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971172_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971172-105">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wellesley arrived in Lisbon on 22 April 1809 on board <a href="/wiki/French_frigate_Surveillante_(1802)" title="French frigate Surveillante (1802)">HMS <i>Surveillante</i></a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971117_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971117-106">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> after narrowly escaping shipwreck.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGriffiths1897_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGriffiths1897-107">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> Reinforced, he took to the offensive. In the <a href="/wiki/Second_Battle_of_Porto" title="Second Battle of Porto">Second Battle of Porto</a> he crossed the <a href="/wiki/Douro" title="Douro">Douro</a> river in a daylight <i><a href="/wiki/Coup_de_main" title="Coup de main">coup de main</a></i>, and routed <a href="/wiki/Nicolas_Jean_de_Dieu_Soult" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicolas Jean de Dieu Soult">Marshal Soult</a>'s French troops in <a href="/wiki/Porto" title="Porto">Porto</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford2012118_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford2012118-108">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With Portugal secured, Wellesley advanced into Spain to unite with <a href="/wiki/Gregorio_de_la_Cuesta" class="mw-redirect" title="Gregorio de la Cuesta">General Cuesta</a>'s forces. The combined allied force prepared for an assault on <a href="/wiki/Claude_Victor-Perrin,_Duc_de_Belluno" class="mw-redirect" title="Claude Victor-Perrin, Duc de Belluno">Marshal Victor</a>'s <a href="/wiki/I_Corps_(Grande_Arm%C3%A9e)" title="I Corps (Grande Armée)">I&#160;Corps</a> at Talavera, 23 July. Cuesta, however, was reluctant to agree, and was only persuaded to advance on the following day.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGates2002177_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGates2002177-109">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> The delay allowed the French to withdraw, but Cuesta sent his army headlong after Victor, and found himself faced by almost the entire French army in New Castile—Victor had been reinforced by the Toledo and Madrid garrisons. The Spanish retreated precipitously, necessitating the advance of two British divisions to cover their retreat.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGuedalla1997186_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuedalla1997186-110">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The next day, 27 July, at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Talavera" title="Battle of Talavera">Battle of Talavera</a> the French advanced in three columns and were repulsed several times throughout the day by Wellesley, but at a heavy cost to the British force. In the aftermath Marshal Soult's army was discovered to be advancing south, threatening to cut Wellesley off from Portugal. Wellesley moved east on 3 August to block it, leaving 1,500 wounded in the care of the Spanish,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford2012134_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford2012134-111">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> intending to confront Soult before finding out that the French were in fact 30,000 strong. The British commander sent the Light Brigade on a dash to hold the bridge over the <a href="/wiki/Tagus" title="Tagus">Tagus</a> at <a href="/wiki/Almaraz" title="Almaraz">Almaraz</a>. With communications and supply from Lisbon secured for now, Wellesley considered joining with Cuesta again but found out that his Spanish ally had abandoned the British wounded to the French and was thoroughly uncooperative, promising and then refusing to supply the British forces, aggravating Wellesley and causing considerable friction between the British and their Spanish allies. The lack of supplies, coupled with the threat of French reinforcement (including the possible inclusion of Napoleon himself) in the spring, led to the British deciding to retreat into Portugal.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford2012134–150_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford2012134–150-112">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following his victory at Talavera, Wellesley was elevated to the Peerage of the United Kingdom on 26 August 1809 as <b>Viscount Wellington</b> of Talavera and of <a href="/wiki/Wellington,_Somerset" title="Wellington, Somerset">Wellington</a>, in the County of Somerset, with the subsidiary title of <b>Baron Douro</b> of Wellesley.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMuir2013343_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMuir2013343-113">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="1810.E2.80.931812"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="1810–1812">1810–1812</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: 1810–1812">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1810, a newly enlarged French army under Marshal <a href="/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Mass%C3%A9na" title="André Masséna">André Masséna</a> invaded Portugal. British opinion was negative and there were suggestions to evacuate Portugal. Instead, Lord Wellington first slowed the French at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Bu%C3%A7aco" class="mw-redirect" title="Battle of Buçaco">Buçaco</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971225–230_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971225–230-115">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> he then prevented them from taking the Lisbon Peninsula by the construction of massive earthworks, known as the <a href="/wiki/Lines_of_Torres_Vedras" title="Lines of Torres Vedras">Lines of Torres Vedras</a>, which had been assembled in complete secrecy with their flanks guarded by the Royal Navy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971235–240_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971235–240-116">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> The baffled and starving French invasion forces retreated after six months. Wellington's pursuit was hindered by a series of reverses inflicted by <a href="/wiki/Michel_Ney" title="Michel Ney">Marshal Ney</a> in a much-lauded <a href="/wiki/Rear_guard" class="mw-redirect" title="Rear guard">rear guard</a> campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford2012163_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford2012163-117">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1811, Masséna returned toward Portugal to relieve Almeida; Wellington narrowly checked the French at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Fuentes_de_O%C3%B1oro" title="Battle of Fuentes de Oñoro">Battle of Fuentes de Oñoro</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971251–254_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971251–254-118">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> Simultaneously, his subordinate, <a href="/wiki/William_Carr_Beresford,_1st_Viscount_Beresford" class="mw-redirect" title="William Carr Beresford, 1st Viscount Beresford">Viscount Beresford</a>, fought Soult's 'Army of the South' to a bloody stalemate at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Albuera" title="Battle of Albuera">Battle of Albuera</a> in May.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971257_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971257-119">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> Wellington was promoted to full <a href="/wiki/General_(United_Kingdom)" title="General (United Kingdom)">general</a> on 31 July for his services. The French abandoned <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Almeida_(1811)" class="mw-redirect" title="Siege of Almeida (1811)">Almeida</a>, avoiding British pursuit,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971254–256_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971254–256-120">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> but retained the twin Spanish fortresses of <a href="/wiki/Ciudad_Rodrigo" title="Ciudad Rodrigo">Ciudad Rodrigo</a> and <a href="/wiki/Badajoz" title="Badajoz">Badajoz</a>, the 'Keys' guarding the roads through the mountain passes into Portugal.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971168_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971168-121">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Wellington_a_Salamanca.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Wellington_a_Salamanca.jpg/220px-Wellington_a_Salamanca.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="159" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Wellington_a_Salamanca.jpg/330px-Wellington_a_Salamanca.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Wellington_a_Salamanca.jpg/440px-Wellington_a_Salamanca.jpg 2x" data-file-width="956" data-file-height="693" /></a><figcaption>Wellington at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Salamanca" title="Battle of Salamanca">Battle of Salamanca</a> (engraving after William Heath)</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1812, Wellington finally captured <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Ciudad_Rodrigo_(1812)" title="Siege of Ciudad Rodrigo (1812)">Ciudad Rodrigo</a> via a rapid movement as the French went into winter quarters, storming it before they could react. He then moved south quickly, besieged the fortress of Badajoz for a month and captured it during the night on 6 April 1812. On viewing the aftermath of the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Badajoz_(1812)" class="mw-redirect" title="Battle of Badajoz (1812)">Storming of Badajoz</a>, Wellington lost his composure and cried at the sight of the British dead in the breaches.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002162_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002162-122">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His army now was a veteran British force reinforced by units of the retrained Portuguese army. Campaigning in Spain, he was made <b>Earl of Wellington</b> in the county of Somerset on 22 February 1812.<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> He routed the French at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Salamanca" title="Battle of Salamanca">Battle of Salamanca</a>, taking advantage of a minor French mispositioning.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971283–287_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971283–287-124">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> The victory liberated the Spanish capital of <a href="/wiki/Madrid" title="Madrid">Madrid</a>. He was later made <b>Marquess of Wellington</b>, in the said county on 18 August 1812.<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wellington attempted to take the vital fortress of <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Burgos" title="Siege of Burgos">Burgos</a>, which linked Madrid to France. He failed, due in part to a lack of siege guns, forcing him into a headlong retreat with the loss of over 2,000 casualties.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGates2002366_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGates2002366-126">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The French abandoned <a href="/wiki/Andalusia" title="Andalusia">Andalusia</a>, and combined the troops of Soult and <a href="/wiki/Auguste_de_Marmont" title="Auguste de Marmont">Marmont</a>. Thus combined, the French outnumbered the British, putting the British forces in a precarious position. Wellington withdrew his army and, joined by the smaller corps under the command of <a href="/wiki/Rowland_Hill,_1st_Viscount_Hill" title="Rowland Hill, 1st Viscount Hill">Rowland Hill</a>, which had been moved to Madrid, began to retreat to Portugal. Marshal Soult declined to attack.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971297–299_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971297–299-127">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Francisco_Goya_-_Portrait_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/Francisco_Goya_-_Portrait_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington.jpg/220px-Francisco_Goya_-_Portrait_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="273" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/Francisco_Goya_-_Portrait_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington.jpg/330px-Francisco_Goya_-_Portrait_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/Francisco_Goya_-_Portrait_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington.jpg/440px-Francisco_Goya_-_Portrait_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3406" data-file-height="4226" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Portrait_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington_(Goya)" class="mw-redirect" title="Portrait of the Duke of Wellington (Goya)"><i>The Duke of Wellington</i></a> by <a href="/wiki/Francisco_Goya" title="Francisco Goya">Francisco Goya</a>, 1812–14</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span id="1813.E2.80.931814"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="1813–1814">1813–1814</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: 1813–1814">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1813, Wellington led a new offensive, this time against the French line of communications. He struck through the hills north of Burgos, the <a href="/wiki/Tr%C3%A1s-os-Montes_(region)" class="mw-redirect" title="Trás-os-Montes (region)">Tras os Montes</a>, and switched his supply line from Portugal to <a href="/wiki/Santander,_Cantabria" class="mw-redirect" title="Santander, Cantabria">Santander</a> on Spain's north coast; this led to the French abandoning Madrid and Burgos. Continuing to outflank the French lines, Wellington caught up with and routed the army of King <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Bonaparte" title="Joseph Bonaparte">Joseph Bonaparte</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Vitoria" title="Battle of Vitoria">Battle of Vitoria</a>, for which he was promoted to <a href="/wiki/Field_marshal_(United_Kingdom)" title="Field marshal (United Kingdom)">field marshal</a> on 21 June.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002189_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002189-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> He personally led a column against the French centre, while other columns commanded by <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Graham,_1st_Baron_Lynedoch" title="Thomas Graham, 1st Baron Lynedoch">Sir Thomas Graham</a>, Rowland Hill and the <a href="/wiki/George_Ramsay,_9th_Earl_of_Dalhousie" title="George Ramsay, 9th Earl of Dalhousie">Earl of Dalhousie</a> looped around the French right and left (this battle became the subject of <a href="/wiki/Ludwig_van_Beethoven" title="Ludwig van Beethoven">Beethoven</a>'s orchestral piece, the <i><a href="/wiki/Wellington%27s_Victory" title="Wellington&#39;s Victory">Wellington's Victory</a></i> (<a href="/wiki/Opus_number" title="Opus number">Opus</a> 91). The British troops broke ranks to loot the abandoned French wagons instead of pursuing the beaten foe. When troops failed to return to their units and began harassing the locals, an enraged Wellington wrote in a now famous despatch to <a href="/wiki/Henry_Bathurst,_3rd_Earl_Bathurst" title="Henry Bathurst, 3rd Earl Bathurst">Earl Bathurst</a>, "We have in the service the scum of the earth as common soldiers".<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although later, when his temper had cooled, he extended his comment to praise the men under his command saying that though many of the men were, "the scum of the earth; it is really wonderful that we should have made them to the fine fellows they are".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaythornthwaite19987_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaythornthwaite19987-130">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After taking the small fortresses of <a href="/wiki/Pamplona" title="Pamplona">Pamplona</a>, Wellington invested <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_San_Sebastian" class="mw-redirect" title="Siege of San Sebastian">San Sebastián</a> but was frustrated by the obstinate French garrison, losing 693 dead and 316 captured in a failed assault and suspending the siege at the end of July. Soult's relief attempt was blocked by the Spanish Army of Galicia at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_San_Marcial" title="Battle of San Marcial">San Marcial</a>, allowing the Allies to consolidate their position and tighten the ring around the city, which fell in September after a second spirited defence.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971332_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971332-131">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> Wellington then forced Soult's demoralised and battered army into a fighting retreat into France, punctuated by battles at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_the_Pyrenees" title="Battle of the Pyrenees">Pyrenees</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_the_Bidassoa_(1813)" class="mw-redirect" title="Battle of the Bidassoa (1813)">Bidassoa</a> and <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Nivelle" title="Battle of Nivelle">Nivelle</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971336_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971336-133">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> Wellington invaded southern France, winning at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_the_Nive" title="Battle of the Nive">the Nive</a> and <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Orthez" title="Battle of Orthez">Orthez</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971342_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971342-135">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> Wellington's final battle against his rival Soult occurred at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Toulouse_(1814)" title="Battle of Toulouse (1814)">Toulouse</a>, where the Allied divisions were badly mauled storming the French <a href="/wiki/Redoubt" title="Redoubt">redoubts</a>, losing some 4,600 men. Despite this momentary victory, news arrived of Napoleon's defeat and abdication<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971344–345_136-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971344–345-136">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> and Soult, seeing no reason to continue the fighting, agreed on a ceasefire with Wellington, allowing Soult to evacuate the city.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford2012228_137-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford2012228-137">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Hailed as the conquering hero by the British, on 3 May 1814 Wellington was made <b>Duke of Wellington</b>, in the county of Somerset, together with the subsidiary title of <b>Marquess Douro</b>, in said County.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMuir2013343_113-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMuir2013343-113">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He received some recognition during his lifetime (the title of "<a href="/wiki/Duke_of_Ciudad_Rodrigo" title="Duke of Ciudad Rodrigo">Duque de Ciudad Rodrigo</a>" and "<a href="/wiki/Grandee" title="Grandee">Grandee of Spain</a>") and the Spanish King Ferdinand VII allowed him to keep part of the works of art from the Royal Collection which he had recovered from the French. His equestrian portrait features prominently in the Monument to the Battle of Vitoria, in present-day Vitoria-Gasteiz.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECornwell2009_139-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECornwell2009-139">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Congress_of_Vienna.PNG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Congress_of_Vienna.PNG/220px-Congress_of_Vienna.PNG" decoding="async" width="220" height="164" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Congress_of_Vienna.PNG/330px-Congress_of_Vienna.PNG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Congress_of_Vienna.PNG/440px-Congress_of_Vienna.PNG 2x" data-file-width="1883" data-file-height="1400" /></a><figcaption>Wellington (far left) alongside <a href="/wiki/Klemens_von_Metternich" title="Klemens von Metternich">Metternich</a>, <a href="/wiki/Charles_Maurice_de_Talleyrand-P%C3%A9rigord" title="Charles Maurice de Talleyrand-Périgord">Talleyrand</a> and other European diplomats at the <a href="/wiki/Congress_of_Vienna" title="Congress of Vienna">Congress of Vienna</a>, 1815 (engraving after <a href="/wiki/Jean-Baptiste_Isabey" title="Jean-Baptiste Isabey">Jean-Baptiste Isabey</a>)</figcaption></figure><p> His popularity in Britain was due to his image and his appearance as well as to his military triumphs. His victory fitted well with the passion and intensity of the <a href="/wiki/Romanticism" title="Romanticism">Romantic</a> movement, with its emphasis on individuality. His personal style influenced the fashions in Britain at the time: his tall, lean figure and his plumed black hat and grand yet classic uniform and white trousers became very popular.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBicât200365–66_140-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBicât200365–66-140">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In late 1814, the Prime Minister wanted him to take command in Canada with the assignment of winning the <a href="/wiki/War_of_1812" title="War of 1812">War of 1812</a> against the United States. Wellesley replied that he would go to America, but he believed that he was needed more in Europe. He stated:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>I think you have no right, from the state of war, to demand any concession of territory from America... You have not been able to carry it into the enemy's territory, notwithstanding your military success, and now undoubted military superiority, and have not even cleared your own territory on the point of attack. You cannot on any principle of equality in negotiation claim a cession of territory except in exchange for other advantages which you have in your power... Then if this reasoning be true, why stipulate for the <a href="/wiki/Uti_possidetis" title="Uti possidetis">uti possidetis</a>? You can get no territory: indeed, the state of your military operations, however creditable, does not entitle you to demand any.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMills192122_141-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMills192122-141">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>He was appointed <a href="/wiki/British_Ambassador_to_France" class="mw-redirect" title="British Ambassador to France">Ambassador to France</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> then took <a href="/wiki/Robert_Stewart,_Viscount_Castlereagh" title="Robert Stewart, Viscount Castlereagh">Lord Castlereagh</a>'s place as first <a href="/wiki/Plenipotentiary" title="Plenipotentiary">plenipotentiary</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Congress_of_Vienna" title="Congress of Vienna">Congress of Vienna</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 2 January 1815, the title of his Knighthood of the Bath was converted to <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Bath" title="Order of the Bath">Knight Grand Cross</a> upon the expansion of that order.<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Hundred_Days">Hundred Days</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Hundred Days">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Facing_Napoleon">Facing Napoleon</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Facing Napoleon">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Hundred_Days" title="Hundred Days">Hundred Days</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Arthur_Wellesley,_first_Duke_of_Wellington._Wellcome_V0048407.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Arthur_Wellesley%2C_first_Duke_of_Wellington._Wellcome_V0048407.jpg/170px-Arthur_Wellesley%2C_first_Duke_of_Wellington._Wellcome_V0048407.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="233" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Arthur_Wellesley%2C_first_Duke_of_Wellington._Wellcome_V0048407.jpg/255px-Arthur_Wellesley%2C_first_Duke_of_Wellington._Wellcome_V0048407.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Arthur_Wellesley%2C_first_Duke_of_Wellington._Wellcome_V0048407.jpg/340px-Arthur_Wellesley%2C_first_Duke_of_Wellington._Wellcome_V0048407.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2300" data-file-height="3148" /></a><figcaption>Engraving of Wellesley by <a href="/wiki/William_Say_(engraver)" title="William Say (engraver)">William Say</a> after <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Phillips" title="Thomas Phillips">Thomas Phillips</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>On 26 February 1815, Napoleon escaped from <a href="/wiki/Elba" title="Elba">Elba</a> and returned to France. He regained control of the country by May and faced a renewed alliance against him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarbero20052_145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarbero20052-145">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> Wellington left Vienna for what became known as the <a href="/wiki/Waterloo_Campaign" class="mw-redirect" title="Waterloo Campaign">Waterloo Campaign</a>. He arrived in the Netherlands to take command of the British-German army and their allied Dutch, all stationed alongside the <a href="/wiki/Prussia" title="Prussia">Prussian</a> forces of Generalfeldmarschall <a href="/wiki/Gebhard_Leberecht_von_Bl%C3%BCcher" title="Gebhard Leberecht von Blücher">Gebhard Leberecht von Blücher</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971396–407_146-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971396–407-146">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Napoleon's strategy was to isolate the Allied and Prussian armies and annihilate each one separately before the Austrians and Russians arrived. In doing so the vast superiority in numbers of the Coalition would be greatly diminished. He would then seek the possibility of peace with Austria and Russia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971410_147-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971410-147">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The French invaded the Netherlands, with Napoleon defeating the Prussians at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Ligny" title="Battle of Ligny">Ligny</a>, and Marshal Ney engaging indecisively with Wellington at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Quatre_Bras" title="Battle of Quatre Bras">Battle of Quatre Bras</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971423–432_148-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971423–432-148">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> The Prussians retreated 18 miles north to <a href="/wiki/Wavre" title="Wavre">Wavre</a> whilst Wellington's Anglo-Allied army withdrew 15 miles north to a site he had noted the previous year as favourable for a battle: the north ridge of a shallow valley on the Brussels road, just south of the small town of <a href="/wiki/Waterloo,_Belgium" title="Waterloo, Belgium">Waterloo</a>. On 17 June there was torrential rain, which severely hampered movement.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHibbert1997175–176_149-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHibbert1997175–176-149">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> and had a considerable effect the next day, 18 June, when the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Waterloo" title="Battle of Waterloo">Battle of Waterloo</a> was fought. This was the first time Wellington had encountered Napoleon; he commanded an Anglo-Dutch-German army that consisted of approximately 73,000 troops, 26,000 of whom were British.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAdkin200137_150-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdkin200137-150">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> Approximately 30 percent of that 26,000 were Irish.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcGreevy2015_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcGreevy2015-151">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Battle_of_Waterloo">Battle of Waterloo</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Battle of Waterloo">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Waterloo" title="Battle of Waterloo">Battle of Waterloo</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Wellington_at_Waterloo_Hillingford.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Wellington_at_Waterloo_Hillingford.jpg/220px-Wellington_at_Waterloo_Hillingford.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="144" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Wellington_at_Waterloo_Hillingford.jpg/330px-Wellington_at_Waterloo_Hillingford.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Wellington_at_Waterloo_Hillingford.jpg/440px-Wellington_at_Waterloo_Hillingford.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1135" data-file-height="745" /></a><figcaption><i>Wellington at Waterloo</i>, by <a href="/wiki/Robert_Alexander_Hillingford" title="Robert Alexander Hillingford">Robert Alexander Hillingford</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The Battle of Waterloo was fought on Sunday 18 June 1815, near Waterloo (at that time in the United Kingdom of the Netherlands, now in Belgium). It commenced with a diversionary attack on <a href="/wiki/Hougoumont" title="Hougoumont">Hougoumont</a> by a division of French soldiers. After a barrage of 80 cannons, the first French infantry attack was launched by <a href="/wiki/Jean-Baptiste_Drouet,_Comte_d%27Erlon" title="Jean-Baptiste Drouet, Comte d&#39;Erlon">Comte D'Erlon</a>'s I Corps. D'Erlon's troops advanced through the Allied centre, resulting in Allied troops in front of the ridge retreating in disorder through the main position. D'Erlon's corps stormed the most fortified Allied position, <a href="/wiki/La_Haye_Sainte" title="La Haye Sainte">La Haye Sainte</a>, but failed to take it. An Allied division under <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Picton" title="Thomas Picton">Thomas Picton</a> met the remainder of D'Erlon's corps head to head, engaging them in an infantry duel in which Picton was killed. During this struggle <a href="/wiki/Henry_Paget,_1st_Marquess_of_Anglesey" title="Henry Paget, 1st Marquess of Anglesey">Lord Uxbridge</a> launched two of his cavalry brigades at the enemy, catching the French infantry off guard, driving them to the bottom of the slope, and capturing two <a href="/wiki/French_Imperial_Eagle" title="French Imperial Eagle">French Imperial Eagles</a>. The charge, however, over-reached itself, and the British cavalry, crushed by fresh French horsemen sent at them by Napoleon, were driven back, suffering tremendous losses.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBecke1911380_152-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBecke1911380-152">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Shortly before 16:00, Marshal Ney noted an apparent withdrawal from Wellington's centre. He mistook the movement of casualties to the rear for the beginnings of a retreat, and sought to exploit it. Ney at this time had few infantry reserves left, as most of the infantry had been committed either to the futile Hougoumont attack or to the defence of the French right. Ney, therefore, tried to break Wellington's centre with a cavalry charge alone.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESiborne1990439_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESiborne1990439-153">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Waterloo-French_cavalry.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/c/c1/Waterloo-French_cavalry.jpg/220px-Waterloo-French_cavalry.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="138" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/c/c1/Waterloo-French_cavalry.jpg/330px-Waterloo-French_cavalry.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/c/c1/Waterloo-French_cavalry.jpg/440px-Waterloo-French_cavalry.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1701" data-file-height="1069" /></a><figcaption>The <i>Grenadiers à Cheval</i>. Napoleon can be seen in the background on a grey horse. A number of different mounts could have been ridden by Napoleon at Waterloo: Ali, Crebère, Désirée, Jaffa, Marie, and Tauris.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESummerville2007315_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESummerville2007315-154">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>At about 16:30, the first Prussian corps arrived. Commanded by <a href="/wiki/Friedrich_Wilhelm_Freiherr_von_B%C3%BClow" title="Friedrich Wilhelm Freiherr von Bülow">Freiherr von Bülow</a>, IV Corps arrived as the French cavalry attack was in full spate. Bülow sent the 15th Brigade to link up with Wellington's left flank in the <a href="/wiki/Frichermont" class="mw-redirect" title="Frichermont">Frichermont</a>–<a href="/wiki/La_Haie,_Lasne" class="mw-redirect" title="La Haie, Lasne">La Haie</a> area while the brigade's horse artillery battery and additional brigade artillery deployed to its left in support.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHofschröer1999117_155-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHofschröer1999117-155">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> Napoleon sent <a href="/wiki/Georges_Mouton" title="Georges Mouton">Lobau</a>'s corps to intercept the rest of Bülow's IV Corps proceeding to <a href="/wiki/Plancenoit" title="Plancenoit">Plancenoit</a>. The 15th Brigade sent Lobau's corps into retreat to the Plancenoit area. Von Hiller's 16th Brigade also pushed forward with six battalions against Plancenoit. Napoleon had dispatched all eight battalions of the Young Guard to reinforce Lobau, who was now seriously pressed by the enemy. Napoleon's Young Guard counter-attacked and, after very hard fighting, secured Plancenoit, but were themselves counter-attacked and driven out.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHofschröer1999122_156-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHofschröer1999122-156">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> Napoleon then resorted to sending two battalions of the Middle and Old Guard into Plancenoit and after ferocious fighting they recaptured the village.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHofschröer1999122_156-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHofschröer1999122-156">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup>
The French cavalry attacked the British infantry squares many times, each at a heavy cost to the French but with few British casualties. Ney himself was displaced from his horse four times.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChandler1987373_157-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChandler1987373-157">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> Eventually, it became obvious, even to Ney, that cavalry alone were achieving little. Belatedly, he organised a combined-arms attack, using Bachelu's division and Tissot's regiment of Foy's division from Reille's II Corps plus those French cavalry that remained in a fit state to fight. This assault was directed along much the same route as the previous heavy cavalry attacks.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAdkin2001361_158-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdkin2001361-158">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Knotel_-_The_storming_of_La_Haye_Sainte.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="The storming of La Haye Sainte by Knötel" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e7/Knotel_-_The_storming_of_La_Haye_Sainte.jpg/220px-Knotel_-_The_storming_of_La_Haye_Sainte.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e7/Knotel_-_The_storming_of_La_Haye_Sainte.jpg/330px-Knotel_-_The_storming_of_La_Haye_Sainte.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e7/Knotel_-_The_storming_of_La_Haye_Sainte.jpg/440px-Knotel_-_The_storming_of_La_Haye_Sainte.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="666" /></a><figcaption>The storming of La Haye Sainte, by <a href="/wiki/Richard_Kn%C3%B6tel" title="Richard Knötel">Richard Knötel</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Meanwhile, at approximately the same time as Ney's combined-arms assault on the centre-right of Wellington's line, Napoleon ordered Ney to capture La Haye Sainte at whatever the cost. Ney accomplished this with what was left of D'Erlon's corps soon after 18:00. Ney then moved <a href="/wiki/Horse_artillery" title="Horse artillery">horse artillery</a> up towards Wellington's centre and began to attack the infantry squares at short-range with <a href="/wiki/Canister_shot" title="Canister shot">canister</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESiborne1990439_153-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESiborne1990439-153">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> This all but destroyed the 27th (Inniskilling) Regiment, and the 30th and 73rd Regiments suffered such heavy losses that they had to combine to form a viable square. Wellington's centre was now on the verge of collapse and wide open to an attack from the French. Luckily for Wellington, Pirch I's and <a href="/wiki/Hans_Ernst_Karl,_Graf_von_Zieten" title="Hans Ernst Karl, Graf von Zieten">Zieten</a>'s corps of the Prussian Army were now at hand. Zieten's corps permitted the two fresh cavalry brigades of Vivian and Vandeleur on Wellington's extreme left to be moved and posted behind the depleted centre. Pirch I Corps then proceeded to support Bülow and together they regained possession of Plancenoit, and once more the Charleroi road was swept by Prussian round shot. The value of this reinforcement is held in high regard.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBecke1911381_159-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBecke1911381-159">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Battle_of_Waterloo,_June_18th_1815_-_The_wars_of_Wellington,_a_narrative_poem_(1819),_before_169_-_BL.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/The_Battle_of_Waterloo%2C_June_18th_1815_-_The_wars_of_Wellington%2C_a_narrative_poem_%281819%29%2C_before_169_-_BL.jpg/220px-The_Battle_of_Waterloo%2C_June_18th_1815_-_The_wars_of_Wellington%2C_a_narrative_poem_%281819%29%2C_before_169_-_BL.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="187" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/The_Battle_of_Waterloo%2C_June_18th_1815_-_The_wars_of_Wellington%2C_a_narrative_poem_%281819%29%2C_before_169_-_BL.jpg/330px-The_Battle_of_Waterloo%2C_June_18th_1815_-_The_wars_of_Wellington%2C_a_narrative_poem_%281819%29%2C_before_169_-_BL.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/The_Battle_of_Waterloo%2C_June_18th_1815_-_The_wars_of_Wellington%2C_a_narrative_poem_%281819%29%2C_before_169_-_BL.jpg/440px-The_Battle_of_Waterloo%2C_June_18th_1815_-_The_wars_of_Wellington%2C_a_narrative_poem_%281819%29%2C_before_169_-_BL.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1504" data-file-height="1277" /></a><figcaption>Wellington at the battle of Waterloo</figcaption></figure>
<p>The French army now fiercely attacked the Coalition all along the line with the culminating point being reached when Napoleon sent forward the Imperial Guard at 19:30. The attack of the Imperial Guards was mounted by five battalions of the Middle Guard, and not by the Grenadiers or Chasseurs of the Old Guard. Marching through a hail of canister and <a href="/wiki/Skirmisher" title="Skirmisher">skirmisher</a> fire and severely outnumbered, the 3,000 or so Middle Guardsmen advanced to the west of La Haye Sainte and proceeded to separate into three distinct attack forces. One, consisting of two battalions of Grenadiers, defeated the Coalition's first line and marched on. <a href="/wiki/David_Hendrik_Chass%C3%A9" title="David Hendrik Chassé">Chassé</a>'s relatively fresh Dutch division was sent against them, and Allied artillery fired into the victorious Grenadiers' flank. This still could not stop the Guard's advance, so Chassé ordered his first brigade to charge the outnumbered French, who faltered and broke.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChesney1907178–179_160-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChesney1907178–179-160">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Plas_Newydd_(Anglesey)_-_Waterloo_1.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/Plas_Newydd_%28Anglesey%29_-_Waterloo_1.jpg/220px-Plas_Newydd_%28Anglesey%29_-_Waterloo_1.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/Plas_Newydd_%28Anglesey%29_-_Waterloo_1.jpg/330px-Plas_Newydd_%28Anglesey%29_-_Waterloo_1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/Plas_Newydd_%28Anglesey%29_-_Waterloo_1.jpg/440px-Plas_Newydd_%28Anglesey%29_-_Waterloo_1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2560" data-file-height="1712" /></a><figcaption>British 10th Hussars of Vivian's Brigade (red shakos – blue uniforms) attacking mixed French troops, including a square of Guard grenadiers (left, middle distance) in the final stages of the battle</figcaption></figure>
<p>Further to the west, 1,500 British Foot Guards under <a href="/wiki/Peregrine_Maitland" title="Peregrine Maitland">Maitland</a> were lying down to protect themselves from the French artillery. As two battalions of Chasseurs approached, the second prong of the Imperial Guard's attack, Maitland's guardsmen rose and devastated them with point-blank volleys. The Chasseurs deployed to counter-attack but began to waver. A bayonet charge by the Foot Guards then broke them. The third prong, a fresh Chasseur battalion, now came up in support. The British guardsmen retreated with these Chasseurs in pursuit, but the latter were halted as the <a href="/wiki/52nd_(Oxfordshire)_Regiment_of_Foot" title="52nd (Oxfordshire) Regiment of Foot">52nd Light Infantry</a> wheeled in line onto their flank and poured a devastating fire into them and then charged.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChesney1907178–179_160-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChesney1907178–179-160">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEParry190070_161-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEParry190070-161">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> Under this onslaught, they too broke.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEParry190070_161-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEParry190070-161">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The last of the Guard retreated headlong. Mass panic ensued through the French lines as the news spread: "<i>La Garde recule. Sauve qui peut!</i>" ("The Guard is retreating. Every man for himself!"). Wellington then stood up in <a href="/wiki/Copenhagen_(horse)" title="Copenhagen (horse)">Copenhagen</a>'s stirrups, and waved his hat in the air to signal an advance of the Allied line just as the Prussians were overrunning the French positions to the east. What remained of the French army then abandoned the field in disorder. Wellington and Blücher met at the inn of La Belle Alliance, on the north–south road which bisected the battlefield, and it was agreed that the Prussians should pursue the retreating French army back to France.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChesney1907178–179_160-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChesney1907178–179-160">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1815)" title="Treaty of Paris (1815)">Treaty of Paris</a> was signed on 20 November 1815.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGreat_Britain_Foreign_Office1838280_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreat_Britain_Foreign_Office1838280-162">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
After the victory, the Duke supported proposals that a medal be awarded to all British soldiers who participated in the Waterloo campaign, and on 28 June 1815, he wrote to the <a href="/wiki/Prince_Frederick,_Duke_of_York_and_Albany" title="Prince Frederick, Duke of York and Albany">Duke of York</a> suggesting:</p><blockquote><p> ... the expediency of giving to the non-commissioned officers and soldiers engaged in the Battle of Waterloo a medal. I am convinced it would have the best effect in the army, and if the battle should settle our concerns, they will well deserve it.</p></blockquote><p>The <a href="/wiki/Waterloo_Medal" title="Waterloo Medal">Waterloo Medal</a> was duly authorised and distributed to all ranks in 1816.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECawthorne201590_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECawthorne201590-163">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup>
</p><h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Controversy">Controversy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Controversy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Much historical discussion has been made about Napoleon's decision to send 33,000 troops under <a href="/wiki/Emmanuel_de_Grouchy,_Marquis_de_Grouchy" class="mw-redirect" title="Emmanuel de Grouchy, Marquis de Grouchy">Marshal Grouchy</a> to intercept the Prussians, but—having defeated Blücher at Ligny on 16 June and forced the Allies to retreat in divergent directions—Napoleon may have been strategically astute in a judgement that he would have been unable to beat the combined Allied forces on one battlefield. Wellington's comparable strategic gamble was to leave 17,000 troops and artillery, mostly Dutch, 8.1&#160;mi (13.0&#160;km) away at Hal, north-west of Mont-Saint-Jean, in case of a French advance up the Mons-Hal-Brussels road.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAdkin200149_164-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdkin200149-164">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Jan_Willem_Pieneman00.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/Jan_Willem_Pieneman00.jpg/260px-Jan_Willem_Pieneman00.jpg" decoding="async" width="260" height="133" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/Jan_Willem_Pieneman00.jpg/390px-Jan_Willem_Pieneman00.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/Jan_Willem_Pieneman00.jpg/520px-Jan_Willem_Pieneman00.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1600" data-file-height="817" /></a><figcaption>Wellington at the battle of Waterloo. Detail of a painting by <a href="/wiki/Jan_Willem_Pieneman" title="Jan Willem Pieneman">Jan Willem Pieneman</a>, 1824.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The campaign led to numerous other controversies. Issues concerning Wellington's troop dispositions prior to Napoleon's invasion of the Netherlands, whether Wellington misled or betrayed Blücher by promising, then failing, to come directly to Blücher's aid at Ligny, and credit for the victory between Wellington and the Prussians. These and other such issues concerning Blücher's, Wellington's, and Napoleon's decisions during the campaign were the subject of a strategic-level study by the Prussian political-military theorist <a href="/wiki/Carl_von_Clausewitz" title="Carl von Clausewitz">Carl von Clausewitz</a>,  <i>Feldzug von 1815: Strategische Uebersicht des Feldzugs von 1815</i>, (English title: <i>The Campaign of 1815: Strategic Overview of the Campaign</i>.)<sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> This study was Clausewitz's last such work and is widely considered to be the best example of Clausewitz's mature theories concerning such analyses.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMoran2010_166-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoran2010-166">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> It attracted the attention of Wellington's staff, who prompted the Duke to write a published essay on the campaign (other than his immediate, official after-action report, "The Waterloo Dispatch".) This was published as the 1842 "Memorandum on the Battle of Waterloo". While Wellington disputed Clausewitz on several points, Clausewitz largely absolved Wellington of accusations levelled against him. This exchange with Clausewitz was quite famous in Britain in the 19th century, particularly in <a href="/wiki/Charles_Cornwallis_Chesney" title="Charles Cornwallis Chesney">Charles Cornwallis Chesney's</a> work the <i>Waterloo Lectures,</i> but was largely ignored in the 20th century due to hostilities between Britain and Germany.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBassford2010_167-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBassford2010-167">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Politics">Politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Politics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister">Prime Minister<span class="anchor" id="First_premiership"></span></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Prime Minister">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Wellington%E2%80%93Peel_ministry" title="Wellington–Peel ministry">Wellington–Peel ministry</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Arthur-Wellesley-1st-Duke-of-Wellington.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Arthur-Wellesley-1st-Duke-of-Wellington.jpg/170px-Arthur-Wellesley-1st-Duke-of-Wellington.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="218" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Arthur-Wellesley-1st-Duke-of-Wellington.jpg/255px-Arthur-Wellesley-1st-Duke-of-Wellington.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Arthur-Wellesley-1st-Duke-of-Wellington.jpg/340px-Arthur-Wellesley-1st-Duke-of-Wellington.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2400" data-file-height="3072" /></a><figcaption>Portrait of the Duke of Wellington by <a href="/wiki/John_Jackson_(painter)" title="John Jackson (painter)">John Jackson</a>, 1830–31</figcaption></figure>

<div class="sidebar-list mw-collapsible mw-collapsed"><div class="sidebar-list-title" style="padding-left:0.4em;padding-right:0.4em;background:#98285c;color:white;">Related topics</div><div class="sidebar-list-content mw-collapsible-content hlist">
