<li><a href="/wiki/Veronese_Easter" title="Veronese Easter">Veronese Easter</a></li></ul></div></div></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Toryism" title="Template:Toryism"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Toryism" title="Template talk:Toryism"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Toryism&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Wellington entered politics again when he was appointed <a href="/wiki/Master-General_of_the_Ordnance" title="Master-General of the Ordnance">Master-General of the Ordnance</a> in the Tory government of <a href="/wiki/Robert_Banks_Jenkinson,_2nd_Earl_of_Liverpool" class="mw-redirect" title="Robert Banks Jenkinson, 2nd Earl of Liverpool">Lord Liverpool</a> on 26 December 1818.<sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> He also became <a href="/wiki/Governor_of_Plymouth" class="mw-redirect" title="Governor of Plymouth">Governor of Plymouth</a> on 9 October 1819.<sup id="cite_ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> He was appointed <a href="/wiki/Commander-in-Chief_of_the_Forces" title="Commander-in-Chief of the Forces">Commander-in-Chief of the British Army</a> on 22 January 1827<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002268_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002268-170">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Constable_of_the_Tower" title="Constable of the Tower">Constable of the Tower of London</a> on 5 February 1827.<sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Along with <a href="/wiki/Robert_Peel" title="Robert Peel">Robert Peel</a>, Wellington became an increasingly influential member of the Tory party, and in 1828 he resigned as Commander-in-Chief and became <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">prime minister</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002270–271_173-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002270–271-173">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During his first seven months as prime minister, he chose not to live in the official residence at <a href="/wiki/10_Downing_Street" title="10 Downing Street">10 Downing Street</a>, finding it too small. He moved in only because his own home, <a href="/wiki/Apsley_House" title="Apsley House">Apsley House</a>, required extensive renovations. During this time he was largely instrumental in the foundation of <a href="/wiki/King%27s_College_London" title="King&#39;s College London">King's College London</a>. On 20 January 1829 Wellington was appointed <a href="/wiki/Lord_Warden_of_the_Cinque_Ports" title="Lord Warden of the Cinque Ports">Lord Warden of the Cinque Ports</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Reform">Reform</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Reform">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Catholic_emancipation">Catholic emancipation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Catholic emancipation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Catholic_emancipation" title="Catholic emancipation">Catholic emancipation</a>, <a href="/wiki/Reform_Bill" class="mw-redirect" title="Reform Bill">Reform Bill</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Emancipation_of_Jews" class="mw-redirect" title="Emancipation of Jews">Emancipation of Jews</a></div>
<p>His term was marked by <a href="/wiki/Catholic_emancipation" title="Catholic emancipation">Roman Catholic Emancipation</a>: the restoration of most civil rights to Roman Catholics in the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland. The change was prompted by the landslide <a href="/wiki/By-election" title="By-election">by-election</a> win of <a href="/wiki/Daniel_O%27Connell" title="Daniel O&#39;Connell">Daniel O'Connell</a>, a Roman Catholic Irish proponent of emancipation, who was elected despite not being legally allowed to sit in Parliament. In the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Lords" title="House of Lords">House of Lords</a>, facing stiff opposition, Wellington spoke for Catholic Emancipation, and according to some sources, gave one of the best speeches of his career.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBloy2011a_175-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBloy2011a-175">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup> Wellington was born in Ireland and so had some understanding of the grievances of the Roman Catholic majority there; as Chief Secretary, he had given an undertaking that the remaining Penal Laws would only be enforced as "mildly" as possible.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971174_99-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971174-99">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> In 1811 Catholic soldiers were given freedom of worship<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHansard1811_176-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHansard1811-176">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> and 18 years later the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Relief_Act_1829" title="Roman Catholic Relief Act 1829">Roman Catholic Relief Act 1829</a> was passed with a majority of 105. Many Tories voted against the Act, and it passed only with the help of the <a href="/wiki/British_Whig_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="British Whig Party">Whigs</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002277_177-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002277-177">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> Wellington had threatened to resign as prime minister if King George IV did not give Royal Assent.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThompson198695_178-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThompson198695-178">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Phillips_An_Eclipse_Lately_Discovered_1829_Cornell_CUL_PJM_1044_01.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/John_Phillips_An_Eclipse_Lately_Discovered_1829_Cornell_CUL_PJM_1044_01.jpg/220px-John_Phillips_An_Eclipse_Lately_Discovered_1829_Cornell_CUL_PJM_1044_01.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="299" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/John_Phillips_An_Eclipse_Lately_Discovered_1829_Cornell_CUL_PJM_1044_01.jpg/330px-John_Phillips_An_Eclipse_Lately_Discovered_1829_Cornell_CUL_PJM_1044_01.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/John_Phillips_An_Eclipse_Lately_Discovered_1829_Cornell_CUL_PJM_1044_01.jpg/440px-John_Phillips_An_Eclipse_Lately_Discovered_1829_Cornell_CUL_PJM_1044_01.jpg 2x" data-file-width="6171" data-file-height="8399" /></a><figcaption>A satirical cartoon attacking the Duke of Wellington, then prime minister, for the passage in April 1829 of the Roman Catholic Relief Act</figcaption></figure>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Duel_with_Winchilsea">Duel with Winchilsea</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: Duel with Winchilsea">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/George_Finch-Hatton,_10th_Earl_of_Winchilsea" title="George Finch-Hatton, 10th Earl of Winchilsea">Earl of Winchilsea</a> accused the Duke of "an insidious design for the infringement of our liberties and the introduction of Popery into every department of the State".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002275_179-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002275-179">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> Wellington responded by immediately challenging Winchilsea to a <a href="/wiki/Duel" title="Duel">duel</a>. On 21 March 1829, Wellington and Winchilsea met on <a href="/wiki/Battersea_Park" title="Battersea Park">Battersea fields</a>. When the time came to fire, the Duke took aim and Winchilsea kept his arm down. The Duke fired wide to the right. Accounts differ as to whether he missed on purpose, an act known in duelling as a <i><a href="/wiki/Delope" class="mw-redirect" title="Delope">delope</a></i>. Wellington claimed he did. However, he was noted for his poor aim and reports more sympathetic to Winchilsea claimed he had aimed to kill. Winchilsea discharged his pistol into the air, a plan he and his second had almost certainly decided upon before the duel.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKing&#39;s_College_London2008_180-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKing&#39;s_College_London2008-180">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup> Honour was saved and Winchilsea wrote Wellington an apology.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002275_179-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002275-179">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELiterary_Gazette1829_181-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELiterary_Gazette1829-181">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The nickname "Iron Duke" originated from this period, when he experienced a high degree of personal and political unpopularity. Its repeated use in <i><a href="/wiki/Freeman%27s_Journal" title="Freeman&#39;s Journal">Freeman's Journal</a></i> throughout June 1830 appears to bear reference to his resolute political will, with taints of disapproval from its Irish editors.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreeman&#39;s_Journal1830a_182-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreeman&#39;s_Journal1830a-182">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreeman&#39;s_Journal1830b_183-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreeman&#39;s_Journal1830b-183">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreeman&#39;s_Journal1830c_184-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreeman&#39;s_Journal1830c-184">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> During this time, Wellington was greeted by a hostile reaction from the crowds at the <a href="/wiki/Opening_of_the_Liverpool_and_Manchester_Railway" title="Opening of the Liverpool and Manchester Railway">opening of the Liverpool and Manchester Railway</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlackwood&#39;s_Edinburgh_Magazine1830_185-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlackwood&#39;s_Edinburgh_Magazine1830-185">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Resignation_and_aftermath">Resignation and aftermath</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=31" title="Edit section: Resignation and aftermath">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Wellington's government fell in 1830. In the summer and autumn of that year, a wave of <a href="/wiki/Swing_Riots" title="Swing Riots">riots</a> swept the country.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002281_186-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002281-186">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup> The Whigs had been out of power for most years since the 1770s, and saw political reform in response to the unrest as the key to their return. Wellington stuck to the Tory policy of no reform and no expansion of suffrage, and as a result, lost a vote of no confidence on 15 November 1830.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002283_187-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002283-187">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Whigs introduced the first <a href="/wiki/Reform_Act_1832" title="Reform Act 1832">Reform Bill</a> while Wellington and the Tories worked to prevent its passage. The Whigs could not get the bill past its second reading in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="House of Commons of the United Kingdom">British House of Commons</a>, and the attempt failed. An election followed in direct response and the Whigs were returned with a landslide majority. A second Reform Act was introduced and passed in the House of Commons but was defeated in the Tory-controlled House of Lords. Another wave of near-insurrection swept the country. Wellington's residence at Apsley House was targeted by a mob of demonstrators on 27 April 1831 and again on 12 October, leaving his windows smashed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBloy2011b_188-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBloy2011b-188">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> Iron shutters were installed in June 1832 to prevent further damage by crowds angry over rejection of the <a href="/wiki/Reform_Bill" class="mw-redirect" title="Reform Bill">Reform Bill</a>, which he strongly opposed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreeman&#39;s_Journal1832_189-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreeman&#39;s_Journal1832-189">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup>  The Whig Government fell in 1832 and Wellington was unable to form a Tory Government partly because of a run on the <a href="/wiki/Bank_of_England" title="Bank of England">Bank of England</a>. This left King William IV no choice but to restore <a href="/wiki/Charles_Grey,_2nd_Earl_Grey" title="Charles Grey, 2nd Earl Grey">Earl Grey</a> to the premiership. Eventually, the bill passed the House of Lords after the King threatened to fill that House with newly created Whig peers if it were not. Wellington was never reconciled to the change; when Parliament first met after the first election under the widened franchise, Wellington is reported to have said "I never saw so many shocking bad hats in my life".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002288_190-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002288-190">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wellington opposed the Jewish Civil Disabilities Repeal Bill, and he stated in Parliament on 1 August 1833 that England "is a Christian country and a Christian legislature, and that the effect of this measure would be to remove that peculiar character." The Bill was defeated by 104 votes to 54.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWellesley1854671–674_191-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWellesley1854671–674-191">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Government">Government<span class="anchor" id="Second_premiership"></span></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=32" title="Edit section: Government">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Wellington_caretaker_ministry" title="Wellington caretaker ministry">Wellington caretaker ministry</a> and <a href="/wiki/First_Peel_ministry" title="First Peel ministry">First Peel ministry</a></div>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Portrait_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington,_1844,_by_Antoine_Claudet.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/Portrait_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington%2C_1844%2C_by_Antoine_Claudet.jpg/220px-Portrait_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington%2C_1844%2C_by_Antoine_Claudet.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="285" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/Portrait_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington%2C_1844%2C_by_Antoine_Claudet.jpg/330px-Portrait_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington%2C_1844%2C_by_Antoine_Claudet.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/Portrait_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington%2C_1844%2C_by_Antoine_Claudet.jpg/439px-Portrait_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington%2C_1844%2C_by_Antoine_Claudet.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1912" data-file-height="2480" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Daguerreotype" title="Daguerreotype">Daguerreotype</a> of Wellington, aged 74 or 75, by <a href="/wiki/Antoine_Claudet" title="Antoine Claudet">Antoine Claudet</a>, 1844</figcaption></figure>
<p>Wellington was gradually superseded as leader of the Tories by Robert Peel, while the party evolved into the Conservatives. When the Tories were returned to power in 1834, Wellington declined to become prime minister because he thought membership in the House of Commons had become essential. The king reluctantly approved Peel, who was in Italy. Hence, Wellington acted as interim leader for three weeks in November and December 1834, taking the responsibilities of prime minister and most of the other ministries.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002289_192-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002289-192">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> In Peel's first cabinet (1834–1835), Wellington became <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Foreign,_Commonwealth_and_Development_Affairs" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of State for Foreign, Commonwealth and Development Affairs">foreign secretary</a>, while in the second (1841–1846) he was a <a href="/wiki/Minister_without_portfolio_(United_Kingdom)" title="Minister without portfolio (United Kingdom)">minister without portfolio</a> and <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_of_Lords" title="Leader of the House of Lords">Leader of the House of Lords</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002291–292_193-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002291–292-193">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup> Wellington was also re-appointed Commander-in-Chief of the British Army on 15 August 1842 following the resignation of <a href="/wiki/Rowland_Hill,_1st_Viscount_Hill" title="Rowland Hill, 1st Viscount Hill">Lord Hill</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wellington served as the leader of the Conservative party in the House of Lords from 1828 to 1846. Some historians have belittled him as a befuddled reactionary, but a consensus in the late 20th century depicts him as a shrewd operator who hid his cleverness behind the façade of a poorly informed old soldier. Wellington worked to transform the Lords from unstinting support of the Crown to an active player in political manoeuvering, with a commitment to the landed aristocracy. He used his London residence as a venue for intimate dinners and private consultations, together with extensive correspondence that kept him in close touch with party leaders in the Commons, and the main persona in the Lords. He gave public rhetorical support to <a href="/wiki/Ultra-Tory" class="mw-redirect" title="Ultra-Tory">Ultra-Tory</a> anti-reform positions, but then deftly changed positions toward the party's centre, especially when Peel needed support from the upper house. Wellington's success was based on the 44 elected peers from Scotland and Ireland, whose elections he controlled.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavis200343–55_195-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavis200343–55-195">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Later_life">Later life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=33" title="Edit section: Later life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Family_2">Family</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=34" title="Edit section: Family">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Portrait_of_the_Field_Marshall_Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington,_by_Robert_Thorburn.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Portrait_of_the_Field_Marshall_Arthur_Wellesley%2C_1st_Duke_of_Wellington%2C_by_Robert_Thorburn.jpg/220px-Portrait_of_the_Field_Marshall_Arthur_Wellesley%2C_1st_Duke_of_Wellington%2C_by_Robert_Thorburn.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="284" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Portrait_of_the_Field_Marshall_Arthur_Wellesley%2C_1st_Duke_of_Wellington%2C_by_Robert_Thorburn.jpg/330px-Portrait_of_the_Field_Marshall_Arthur_Wellesley%2C_1st_Duke_of_Wellington%2C_by_Robert_Thorburn.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Portrait_of_the_Field_Marshall_Arthur_Wellesley%2C_1st_Duke_of_Wellington%2C_by_Robert_Thorburn.jpg/440px-Portrait_of_the_Field_Marshall_Arthur_Wellesley%2C_1st_Duke_of_Wellington%2C_by_Robert_Thorburn.jpg 2x" data-file-width="464" data-file-height="598" /></a><figcaption>Portrait miniature of an elderly Wellington, by <a href="/wiki/Robert_Thorburn_(painter)" title="Robert Thorburn (painter)">Robert Thorburn</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Wellesley was married by his brother Gerald, a clergyman, to <a href="/wiki/Kitty_Pakenham" class="mw-redirect" title="Kitty Pakenham">Kitty Pakenham</a> in <a href="/wiki/St_George%27s_Church,_Dublin" class="mw-redirect" title="St George&#39;s Church, Dublin">St George's Church, Dublin</a> on 10 April 1806.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes200296_196-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes200296-196">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> They had two children: <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Wellesley,_2nd_Duke_of_Wellington" title="Arthur Wellesley, 2nd Duke of Wellington">Arthur</a> was born in 1807 and <a href="/wiki/Lord_Charles_Wellesley" title="Lord Charles Wellesley">Charles</a> was born in 1808. The marriage proved unsatisfactory and the two spent years apart, while Wellesley was campaigning and afterwards. Kitty grew depressed, and Wellesley pursued other sexual and romantic partners.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENeillands200339_100-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENeillands200339-100">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBBC2015_197-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBBC2015-197">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> The couple largely lived apart, with Kitty spending most of her time at their country home, <a href="/wiki/Stratfield_Saye_House" title="Stratfield Saye House">Stratfield Saye House</a> and Wellesley at their London home, <a href="/wiki/Apsley_House" title="Apsley House">Apsley House</a>. Kitty's brother <a href="/wiki/Edward_Pakenham" title="Edward Pakenham">Edward Pakenham</a> served under Wellesley throughout the <a href="/wiki/Peninsular_War" title="Peninsular War">Peninsular War</a>, and Wellesley's regard for him helped to smooth his relations with Kitty, until Pakenham's death at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_New_Orleans" title="Battle of New Orleans">Battle of New Orleans</a> in 1815.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002206_198-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002206-198">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Retirement">Retirement</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=35" title="Edit section: Retirement">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Wellington retired from political life in 1846, although he remained <a href="/wiki/Commander-in-Chief_of_the_Forces" title="Commander-in-Chief of the Forces">Commander-in-Chief</a>, and returned briefly to the public eye in 1848 when he helped organise a military force to protect London during the <a href="/wiki/Revolutions_of_1848" title="Revolutions of 1848">year of European revolution</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002292_199-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002292-199">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup> The Conservative Party had split over the repeal of the <a href="/wiki/Corn_Laws" title="Corn Laws">Corn Laws</a> in 1846, with Wellington and most of the former Cabinet still supporting Peel, but most of the MPs led by <a href="/wiki/Edward_Smith-Stanley,_14th_Earl_of_Derby" title="Edward Smith-Stanley, 14th Earl of Derby">Lord Derby</a> supporting a protectionist stance. Early in 1852 Wellington, by then very deaf, gave <a href="/wiki/Who%3F_Who%3F_ministry" title="Who? Who? ministry">Derby's first government</a> its nickname by shouting "Who? Who?" as the list of inexperienced Cabinet ministers was read out in the House of Lords.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBloy2011c_200-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBloy2011c-200">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> He became Chief Ranger and Keeper of <a href="/wiki/Hyde_Park,_London" title="Hyde Park, London">Hyde Park</a> and <a href="/wiki/St_James%27s_Park" title="St James&#39;s Park">St James's Park</a> on 31 August 1850.<sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> He remained colonel of the <a href="/wiki/Duke_of_Wellington%27s_Regiment" title="Duke of Wellington&#39;s Regiment">33rd Regiment of Foot</a> from 1 February 1806<sup id="cite_ref-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-202">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup> and colonel of the <a href="/wiki/Grenadier_Guards" title="Grenadier Guards">Grenadier Guards</a> from 22 January 1827.<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> Kitty died of cancer in 1831; despite their generally unhappy relations, which had led to an effective separation, Wellington was said to have been greatly saddened by her death, his one comfort being that after "half a lifetime together, they had come to understand each other at the end".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971281_204-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971281-204">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> He had found consolation for his unhappy marriage in his warm friendship with the diarist <a href="/wiki/Harriet_Arbuthnot" title="Harriet Arbuthnot">Harriet Arbuthnot</a>, wife of his colleague <a href="/wiki/Charles_Arbuthnot" title="Charles Arbuthnot">Charles Arbuthnot</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford197195_205-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford197195-205">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup> Harriet's death in the <a href="/wiki/Cholera" title="Cholera">cholera</a> epidemic of 1834 was almost as great a blow to Wellington as it was to her husband.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971296_206-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971296-206">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup> The two widowers spent their last years together at Apsley House.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971297_207-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971297-207">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Death_and_funeral">Death and funeral</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=36" title="Edit section: Death and funeral">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Funeral_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington._The_funeral_car_passing_the_archway_at_Apsley_House,_18_November_1852_22931.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/26/Funeral_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington._The_funeral_car_passing_the_archway_at_Apsley_House%2C_18_November_1852_22931.jpg/220px-Funeral_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington._The_funeral_car_passing_the_archway_at_Apsley_House%2C_18_November_1852_22931.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="179" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/26/Funeral_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington._The_funeral_car_passing_the_archway_at_Apsley_House%2C_18_November_1852_22931.jpg/330px-Funeral_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington._The_funeral_car_passing_the_archway_at_Apsley_House%2C_18_November_1852_22931.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/26/Funeral_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington._The_funeral_car_passing_the_archway_at_Apsley_House%2C_18_November_1852_22931.jpg/440px-Funeral_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington._The_funeral_car_passing_the_archway_at_Apsley_House%2C_18_November_1852_22931.jpg 2x" data-file-width="960" data-file-height="782" /></a><figcaption>Wellesley's funeral procession passing <a href="/wiki/Wellington_Arch" title="Wellington Arch">Wellington Arch</a> and <a href="/wiki/Apsley_House" title="Apsley House">Apsley House</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Wellington_QE4_120.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Wellington_QE4_120.jpg/220px-Wellington_QE4_120.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Wellington_QE4_120.jpg/330px-Wellington_QE4_120.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Wellington_QE4_120.jpg/440px-Wellington_QE4_120.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3261" data-file-height="2186" /></a><figcaption>Wellington's tomb, in <a href="/wiki/St_Paul%27s_Cathedral" title="St Paul&#39;s Cathedral">St Paul's Cathedral</a>, London</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:WellingtonWoodhouse.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ae/WellingtonWoodhouse.jpg/220px-WellingtonWoodhouse.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="298" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ae/WellingtonWoodhouse.jpg/330px-WellingtonWoodhouse.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ae/WellingtonWoodhouse.jpg/440px-WellingtonWoodhouse.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1302" data-file-height="1764" /></a><figcaption>A bronze statue of Wellington by <a href="/wiki/Carlo_Marochetti" title="Carlo Marochetti">Carlo Marochetti</a> in <a href="/wiki/Woodhouse_Moor" title="Woodhouse Moor">Woodhouse Moor</a>, <a href="/wiki/Leeds" title="Leeds">Leeds</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_the_Duke_of_Wellington" title="Death and state funeral of the Duke of Wellington">Death and state funeral of the Duke of Wellington</a></div>
<p>Wellington died at <a href="/wiki/Walmer_Castle" title="Walmer Castle">Walmer Castle</a> in Kent, his residence as <a href="/wiki/Lord_Warden_of_the_Cinque_Ports" title="Lord Warden of the Cinque Ports">Lord Warden of the Cinque Ports</a> and reputedly his favourite home, on 14 September 1852. He was found to be unwell on that morning and was helped from his campaign bed, which he had used throughout his military career, and seated in his chair where he died. His death was recorded as being due to the after-effects of a stroke culminating in a series of <a href="/wiki/Epileptic_seizure" class="mw-redirect" title="Epileptic seizure">seizures</a>. He was aged 83.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECorrigan2006353_208-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECorrigan2006353-208">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBloy2011b_188-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBloy2011b-188">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although in life he hated travelling by rail, having witnessed the death of <a href="/wiki/William_Huskisson" title="William Huskisson">William Huskisson</a>, one of the first railway accident casualties, his body was taken by train to <a href="/wiki/London" title="London">London</a>, where he was given a <a href="/wiki/State_funerals_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="State funerals in the United Kingdom">state funeral</a> – one of a small number of British subjects to be so honoured (other examples include <a href="/wiki/Horatio_Nelson,_1st_Viscount_Nelson" title="Horatio Nelson, 1st Viscount Nelson">Lord Nelson</a> and Sir <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a>). The funeral took place on 18 November 1852.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThe_Times1852a_209-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThe_Times1852a-209">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-210" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-210">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> Before the funeral, the Duke's body lay in state at the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Hospital_Chelsea" title="Royal Hospital Chelsea">Royal Hospital Chelsea</a>. Members of the royal family, including Queen Victoria, the Prince Consort, the Prince of Wales, and the Princess Royal, visited to pay their respects. When viewing opened to the public, crowds thronged to visit and several people were killed in the crush.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWalford1878_211-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalford1878-211">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He was buried in <a href="/wiki/St_Paul%27s_Cathedral" title="St Paul&#39;s Cathedral">St Paul's Cathedral</a>, and during his funeral, there was little space to stand due to the number of attendees.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002297–298_212-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002297–298-212">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup> A bronze memorial was sculpted by <a href="/wiki/Alfred_Stevens_(sculptor)" title="Alfred Stevens (sculptor)">Alfred Stevens</a>, and features two intricate supports: "Truth tearing the tongue out of the mouth of False-hood", and "Valour trampling Cowardice underfoot". Stevens did not live to see it placed in its home under one of the arches of the cathedral.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVictoria_and_Albert_Museum2014_213-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVictoria_and_Albert_Museum2014-213">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wellington's casket was decorated with banners which were made for his funeral procession. Originally, there was one from Prussia, which was removed during <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a> and never reinstated.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESaint_Paul&#39;s_Cathedral2011_214-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESaint_Paul&#39;s_Cathedral2011-214">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup> In the procession, the "Great Banner" was carried by General <a href="/wiki/James_Charles_Chatterton" title="James Charles Chatterton">Sir James Charles Chatterton</a> of the <a href="/wiki/4th_Royal_Irish_Dragoon_Guards" title="4th Royal Irish Dragoon Guards">4th Dragoon Guards</a> on the orders of <a href="/wiki/Queen_Victoria" title="Queen Victoria">Queen Victoria</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDalton190477_215-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDalton190477-215">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Most of the book <i>A Biographical Sketch of the Military and Political Career of the Late Duke of Wellington</i> by Weymouth newspaper proprietor <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Drew" title="Joseph Drew">Joseph Drew</a> is a detailed contemporary account of his death, <a href="/wiki/Lying_in_state" title="Lying in state">lying in state</a> and funeral.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDrew1852_216-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrew1852-216">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After his death, Irish and English newspapers disputed whether Wellington had been born an Irishman or an Englishman.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESinnema200693–111_217-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESinnema200693–111-217">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> In 2002, he was number 15 in the <a href="/wiki/BBC" title="BBC">BBC</a>'s poll of the <a href="/wiki/100_Greatest_Britons" title="100 Greatest Britons">100 Greatest Britons</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECooperBoycott200213_218-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECooperBoycott200213-218">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Owing to its links with Wellington, as the former commanding officer and colonel of the regiment, the title "33rd (The Duke of Wellington's) Regiment" was granted to the 33rd Regiment of Foot, on 18 June 1853 (the 38th anniversary of the Battle of Waterloo) by Queen Victoria.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuke_of_Wellington&#39;s_Regiment2014_219-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuke_of_Wellington&#39;s_Regiment2014-219">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup> Wellington's <a href="/wiki/Military_career_of_Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington" title="Military career of Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington">battle record</a> is exemplary; he participated in some 60 battles during the course of his military career.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERoberts2003b_220-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERoberts2003b-220">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personality">Personality</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=37" title="Edit section: Personality">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Arthur_Wellesley_(1769%E2%80%931852),_1st_Duke_of_Wellington.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Arthur_Wellesley_%281769%E2%80%931852%29%2C_1st_Duke_of_Wellington.jpg/220px-Arthur_Wellesley_%281769%E2%80%931852%29%2C_1st_Duke_of_Wellington.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="374" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Arthur_Wellesley_%281769%E2%80%931852%29%2C_1st_Duke_of_Wellington.jpg/330px-Arthur_Wellesley_%281769%E2%80%931852%29%2C_1st_Duke_of_Wellington.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Arthur_Wellesley_%281769%E2%80%931852%29%2C_1st_Duke_of_Wellington.jpg/440px-Arthur_Wellesley_%281769%E2%80%931852%29%2C_1st_Duke_of_Wellington.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1051" data-file-height="1787" /></a><figcaption>The Duke of Wellington, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1850</span></figcaption></figure>
<p>Wellington always rose early; he "couldn't bear to lie awake in bed", even if the army was not on the march.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002177_221-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002177-221">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup> Even when he returned to civilian life after 1815, he slept in a camp bed, reflecting his lack of regard for creature comforts. General <a href="/wiki/Miguel_de_%C3%81lava" class="mw-redirect" title="Miguel de Álava">Miguel de Álava</a> complained that Wellington said so often that the army would march "at daybreak" and dine on "cold meat" that he began to dread those two phrases.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002175_222-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002175-222">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup> While on campaign, he seldom ate anything between breakfast and dinner. During the retreat to Portugal in 1811, he subsisted on "cold meat and bread", to the despair of his staff who dined with him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHibbert1997111_223-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHibbert1997111-223">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup> He was, however, renowned for the quality of the wine that he drank and served, often drinking a bottle with his dinner (not a great quantity by the standards of his day).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971356_224-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971356-224">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Álava was a witness to an incident just before the Battle of Salamanca. Wellington was eating a chicken leg while observing the manoeuvres of the French army through a <a href="/wiki/Telescope" title="Telescope">spyglass</a>. He spotted an overextension in the French left flank, and realised that he could launch a successful attack there. He exclaimed "By God, that will do!" and threw the drumstick in the air.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002166_225-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002166-225">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup> After the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Toulouse_(1814)" title="Battle of Toulouse (1814)">Battle of Toulouse</a>, Colonel <a href="/wiki/Frederick_Cavendish_Ponsonby" title="Frederick Cavendish Ponsonby">Frederick Ponsonby</a> brought him the news of Napoleon's abdication, and Wellington broke into an impromptu <a href="/wiki/Flamenco" title="Flamenco">flamenco</a> dance, spinning around on his heels and clicking his fingers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGlover2001334_226-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGlover2001334-226">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:BLW_Model_for_Wellington_Monument_-_Valour_and_Cowardice.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/BLW_Model_for_Wellington_Monument_-_Valour_and_Cowardice.jpg/220px-BLW_Model_for_Wellington_Monument_-_Valour_and_Cowardice.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/BLW_Model_for_Wellington_Monument_-_Valour_and_Cowardice.jpg/330px-BLW_Model_for_Wellington_Monument_-_Valour_and_Cowardice.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/BLW_Model_for_Wellington_Monument_-_Valour_and_Cowardice.jpg/440px-BLW_Model_for_Wellington_Monument_-_Valour_and_Cowardice.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2448" data-file-height="3264" /></a><figcaption>Plaster model, located at the <a href="/wiki/Victoria_and_Albert_Museum" title="Victoria and Albert Museum">Victoria and Albert Museum</a>, of the 'Valour and Cowardice' motif used in the memorial to Wellington at <a href="/wiki/St._Paul%27s_Cathedral" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Paul&#39;s Cathedral">St. Paul's Cathedral</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Military historian Charles Dalton recorded that, after a hard-fought battle in Spain, a young officer made the comment, "I am going to dine with Wellington tonight", which was overheard by the Duke as he rode by. "Give me at least the prefix of Mr. before my name," Wellington said. "My Lord," replied the officer, "we do not speak of Mr. Caesar or Mr. Alexander, so why should I speak of Mr. Wellington?"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDalton19049_227-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDalton19049-227">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While known for his stern countenance and iron-handed discipline, Wellington was by no means unfeeling. While he is said to have disapproved of soldiers cheering as "too nearly an expression of opinion",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGere19815_228-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGere19815-228">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup> Wellington nevertheless cared for his men: he refused to pursue the French after the battles of Porto and Salamanca, foreseeing an inevitable cost to his army in chasing a diminished enemy through rough terrain. The only time that he ever showed grief in public was after the storming of Badajoz: he cried at the sight of the British dead in the breaches.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002162_122-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002162-122">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> In this context, his famous despatch after the Battle of Vitoria, calling them the "scum of the earth", can be seen to be fuelled as much by disappointment at their breaking ranks as by anger. He shed tears after Waterloo on the presentation of the list of British fallen by John Hume. Later with his family, unwilling to be congratulated for his victory, he broke down in tears, his fighting spirit diminished by the high cost of the battle and great personal loss.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002250,_254_229-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002250,_254-229">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wellington's soldier servant, a gruff German called Beckerman, and his long-serving valet, James Kendall, who served him for 25 years and was with him when he died, were both devoted to him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002301_230-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002301-230">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup> (A story that he never spoke to his servants and preferred instead to write his orders on a notepad on his dressing table in fact probably refers to his son, the 2nd Duke. It was recorded by the 3rd Duke's niece, Viva Seton Montgomerie (1879–1959), as being an anecdote she heard from an old retainer, Charles Holman, who was said greatly to resemble Napoleon.)<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMontgomerie195531_231-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMontgomerie195531-231">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following an incident when, as Master-General of the Ordnance he had been close to a large explosion, Wellington began to experience deafness and other ear-related problems. In 1822, he had an operation to improve the hearing of the left ear. The result, however, was that he became permanently deaf on that side. It is claimed that he was "never quite well afterwards".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBloy2011b_188-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBloy2011b-188">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wellington came to enjoy the company of a variety of intellectual and attractive women and had many amorous liaisons, particularly after the Battle of Waterloo and his subsequent ambassadorial position in Paris. In the days following Waterloo he had an affair with the notorious <a href="/wiki/Lady_Caroline_Lamb" title="Lady Caroline Lamb">Lady Caroline Lamb</a>, sister of one of his severely wounded officers and favourites, Col Frederick Ponsonby. He corresponded for many years with Lady Georgiana Lennox, later Lady de Ros, 26 years his junior and daughter of the Duchess of Richmond (who held the famous ball on the eve of Waterloo) and, though there are hints, it has not been clearly determined if the relationship was ever sexual.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrossland201613_232-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossland201613-232">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup> The British press lampooned the amorous side of the national hero.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBBC2015_197-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBBC2015-197">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> In 1824, one liaison came back to haunt him, when Wellington received a letter from a publisher, <a href="/wiki/John_Joseph_Stockdale" title="John Joseph Stockdale">John Joseph Stockdale</a>, offering to refrain from issuing an edition of the rather racy memoirs of one of his mistresses, <a href="/wiki/Harriette_Wilson" title="Harriette Wilson">Harriette Wilson</a>, in exchange for money. It is said that the Duke promptly returned the letter, after scrawling across it, "Publish and be damned".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECathcart1994_233-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECathcart1994-233">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup> However, Hibbert notes in his biography that the letter can be found among the Duke's papers, with nothing written on it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHibbert1997390_234-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHibbert1997390-234">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup> It is certain that Wellington <em>did</em> reply, and the tone of a further letter from the publisher, quoted by Longford, suggests that he had refused in the strongest language to submit to blackmail.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELongford1971211–212_235-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELongford1971211–212-235">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He was also a remarkably practical man who spoke concisely. In 1851, it was discovered that there were a great many sparrows flying about in <a href="/wiki/The_Crystal_Palace" title="The Crystal Palace">the Crystal Palace</a> just before <a href="/wiki/The_Great_Exhibition" class="mw-redirect" title="The Great Exhibition">the Great Exhibition</a> was to open. His advice to Queen Victoria was "Sparrowhawks, ma'am".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002xvi_236-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002xvi-236">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Wellington has often been portrayed as a defensive general, even though many, perhaps most, of his battles were offensive (Argaum, Assaye, Oporto, Salamanca, Vitoria, Toulouse). However, for most of the Peninsular War, where he earned his fame, his army lacked the numbers for a strategically offensive posture.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERothenberg1999136_237-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERothenberg1999136-237">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Titles_and_tributes">Titles and tributes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=38" title="Edit section: Titles and tributes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/List_of_titles_and_honours_of_Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington" title="List of titles and honours of Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington">List of titles and honours of Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Batons_of_Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington" title="Batons of Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington">Batons of Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/List_of_monuments_to_Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington" title="List of monuments to Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington">List of monuments to Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Cultural_depictions_of_Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington" title="Cultural depictions of Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington">Cultural depictions of Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington</a></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Nicknames">Nicknames</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=39" title="Edit section: Nicknames">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Apsley_House_en_1829.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/Apsley_House_en_1829.JPG/220px-Apsley_House_en_1829.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="142" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/Apsley_House_en_1829.JPG/330px-Apsley_House_en_1829.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/Apsley_House_en_1829.JPG/440px-Apsley_House_en_1829.JPG 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="388" /></a><figcaption>Apsley House in 1829, by <a href="/wiki/Thomas_H._Shepherd" title="Thomas H. Shepherd">Thomas H. Shepherd</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Edinburgh-Wellington-wyrdlight.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/Edinburgh-Wellington-wyrdlight.jpg/220px-Edinburgh-Wellington-wyrdlight.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="220" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/Edinburgh-Wellington-wyrdlight.jpg/330px-Edinburgh-Wellington-wyrdlight.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/Edinburgh-Wellington-wyrdlight.jpg/440px-Edinburgh-Wellington-wyrdlight.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2784" data-file-height="2784" /></a><figcaption>Sir John Steell's equestrian statue of the Duke of Wellington – 'the Iron Duke in bronze by Steell', Edinburgh, 1852</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="The_Iron_Duke">The Iron Duke</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=40" title="Edit section: The Iron Duke">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">This section is about a nickname of Wellington. For the metaphor it is based upon, see <a href="/wiki/Iron_(metaphor)" title="Iron (metaphor)">Iron (metaphor)</a>. For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Iron_Duke_(disambiguation)" class="mw-redirect mw-disambig" title="Iron Duke (disambiguation)">Iron Duke (disambiguation)</a>.</div>
<p>This commonly used nickname originally related to his consistent political resolve rather than to any particular incident. In various cases its editorial use appears to be disparaging.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreeman&#39;s_Journal1830a_182-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreeman&#39;s_Journal1830a-182">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreeman&#39;s_Journal1830b_183-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreeman&#39;s_Journal1830b-183">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreeman&#39;s_Journal1830c_184-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreeman&#39;s_Journal1830c-184">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It is likely that its use became more widespread after an incident in 1832 in which he installed metal shutters to prevent rioters breaking windows at Apsley House.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreeman&#39;s_Journal1832_189-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreeman&#39;s_Journal1832-189">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-BBC_History_238-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BBC_History-238">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup> The term may have been made increasingly popular by <i><a href="/wiki/Punch_(magazine)" title="Punch (magazine)">Punch</a></i> cartoons published in 1844–45.<sup id="cite_ref-239" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-239">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002285–288,_302–303_240-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002285–288,_302–303-240">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_nicknames">Other nicknames</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=41" title="Edit section: Other nicknames">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li>In the popular ballads of the day Wellington was called <i>"Nosey"</i> or <i>"Old Nosey"</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-241" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-241">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Tsar" title="Tsar">Tsar</a> <a href="/wiki/Alexander_I_of_Russia" title="Alexander I of Russia">Alexander I of Russia</a> called Wellington "<i>Le vainqueur du vainqueur du monde</i>", the conqueror of the world's conqueror, the phrase "the world's conqueror" referring to <a href="/wiki/Napoleon" title="Napoleon">Napoleon</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-242" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-242">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Alfred,_Lord_Tennyson" title="Alfred, Lord Tennyson">Lord Tennyson</a> uses a similar reference in his "Ode on the Death of the Duke of Wellington", referring to him as "the great World-victor's victor".<sup id="cite_ref-243" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-243">&#91;241&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Officers under his command called him <i>"The Beau"</i>, as he was a fine dresser.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002178_244-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002178-244">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Spanish troops called him <i>"The Eagle"</i>, while Portuguese troops called him <i>"<a href="/wiki/Douro" title="Douro">Douro</a> Douro"</i> after his river crossing at Oporto in 1809.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorgan2004135_245-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorgan2004135-245">&#91;243&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i>"Beau Douro"</i>; Wellington found this amusing when hearing it used by a Colonel of the Coldstream Guards.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002178_244-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolmes2002178-244">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i>"Sepoy General"</i>; Napoleon used this term as an insult to Wellington's military service in India, publicly considering him an unworthy opponent. The name was used in the French newspaper <i><a href="/wiki/Le_Moniteur_Universel" title="Le Moniteur Universel">Le Moniteur Universel</a></i>, as a means of propaganda.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERoberts2003a74,_78–79_246-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERoberts2003a74,_78–79-246">&#91;244&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i>"The Beef"</i>; It is a theory that the <a href="/wiki/Beef_Wellington" title="Beef Wellington">Beef Wellington</a> dish is a reference to Wellington, although some chefs dispute this.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScottWright200626_247-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScottWright200626-247">&#91;245&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i>"Europe's Liberator"</i><sup id="cite_ref-Wellington_248-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wellington-248">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i>"Saviour of the Nations"</i><sup id="cite_ref-Wellington_248-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wellington-248">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="See_also">See also</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=42" title="Edit section: See also">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="References">References</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=43" title="Edit section: References">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington&amp;action=edit&amp;section=44" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-columns references-column-width reflist-lower-alpha" style="column-width: 32em;">
<ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-8"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-8">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Though 29 April is considered as more likely by some other earlier biographers, including <a href="/wiki/Ernest_Marsh_Lloyd" title="Ernest Marsh Lloyd">Ernest Marsh Lloyd</a>, writing in the <i>Dictionary of National Biography</i> with some sources to support that view, <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELloyd1899170_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELloyd1899170-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Norman Gash writing in the more modern <i>Oxford Dictionary of National Biography</i>, on balance supports 1 May.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGash2011_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGash2011-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-165"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-165">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">see <a href="#CITEREFBassfordMoranPedlow2010">Bassford, Moran &amp; Pedlow (2010)</a> for details..</span>
</li>

<li id="cite_note-125"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-125">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFGazette16636" class="citation magazine cs1 cs1-prop-jul-greg-uncertainty"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.thegazette.co.uk/London/issue/16636/page/1677">"No. 16636"</a>. <i><a href="/wiki/The_London_Gazette" title="The London Gazette">The London Gazette</a></i>. 18 August 1812. p.&#160;1677.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=article&amp;rft.jtitle=The+London+Gazette&amp;rft.atitle=No.+16636&amp;rft.pages=1677&amp;rft.date=1812-08-18&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.thegazette.co.uk%2FLondon%2Fissue%2F16636%2Fpage%2F1677&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AArthur+Wellesley%2C+1st+Duke+of+Wellington" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-FOOTNOTEGates2002366-126"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGates2002366_126-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFGates2002">Gates (2002)</a>, p.&#160;366: "While, in view of the developing strategic situation, is not clear what Wellesley hoped to gain by its seizure, he had resolved to take the fortress—a task which he evidently believed could be easily accomplished; for, notwithstanding the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Badajoz_(1812)" class="mw-redirect" title="Battle of Badajoz (1812)">sanguinary lessons</a> that virtually all his sieges had given him and the availability of scores of heavy cannon captured at Ciudad Rodrigo and Madrid, he brought up only eight heavy guns to breach the defences. This force was to prove lamentably inadequate and, in this and other aspects of the operation, Wellesley's complacency and ineptitude were to cost his troops dear."</span>
</li>
